# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-10-30

## Time Series

| **[Timestral](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37585v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce Timestral, a family of four time series foundation models for probabilistic forecasting, with 5M, 28M, 123M, and 209M parameters. Timestral sets a new state of the art on GIFT-Eval, TIME, and fev-bench, where Timestral-209M and Timestral-123M take the top two places on every metric. We achieve this with three ingredients: Decoupled Horizon Forecasting (DHF), which separates prediction tokens from the causally encoded context, so that longer horizons are trained without masking context and each observed value is both a forecast target and context for later forecasts; a covariate framework that natively supports known-future covariates within a causal architecture; and a two-stage pipeline that pretrains exclusively on a diverse, large-scale synthetic corpus before post-training on a mix of real and synthetic data with extended context. This report describes the model design and its benchmark results. Weights for all four models are available on Hugging Face and inference code on GitHub.</p></details> | <details><summary>Webpa...</summary><p>Webpage: https://mistral.ai/news/timestral ; Weights: https://huggingface.co/collections/mistralai/timestral ; Code: https://github.com/mistralai/timestral</p></details> |
| **[Pneuma: A Large-Scale Real-World Time Series Benchmark for Commercial Aircraft Predictive Maintenance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37563v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Predictive maintenance is vital for commercial aviation, yet progress remains hindered by the lack of public, large-scale real-world datasets. As a result, most methods are developed and evaluated on proprietary data, limiting reproducibility. We introduce Pneuma, the first publicly available large-scale benchmark for predictive maintenance using onboard time series from in-service commercial aircraft. Built around bleed air systems, Pneuma contains over 920 million multivariate time steps across four aircraft types and 9 verified fault scenarios, directly aligned with real maintenance logs. The dataset exhibits key real-world challenges, including substantial class imbalance, subtle fault evolution patterns, and significant variance across aircraft configurations. We structure the benchmark around three tasks: multivariate forecasting, supervised fault classification, and unsupervised anomaly detection, with labels derived from actual maintenance records. Under unified settings, we evaluate over 25 models from four methodological families on 9 carefully curated subsets. The results reveal that even strong contemporary models struggle with subtle fault signatures. Further analyses show consistent gains from additional in-context examples and multivariate inference, while forecasting quality is strongly associated with anomaly detection performance. Together, these findings provide practical insights into effectively modeling complex real-world aviation time series. We hope Pneuma will serve as a rigorous and reproducible foundation for advancing data-driven predictive maintenance toward real-world deployment in commercial aviation.</p></details> |  |
| **[BadTime: An Effective Backdoor Attack on Multivariate Long-Term Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.04189v3)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multivariate long-term time series forecasting (MLTSF) models are increasingly deployed in critical domains such as climate, finance, and transportation. Despite their growing importance, the security of MLTSF models against backdoor attacks remains entirely unexplored. To bridge this gap, we propose BadTime, the first effective backdoor attack tailored for MLTSF. BadTime can manipulate hundreds of future predictions toward a target pattern by injecting a subtle trigger. BadTime addresses two key challenges that become particularly severe in MLTSF: (i) the rapid dilution of local triggers over long horizons, and (ii) the extreme sparsity of backdoor signals under stealth constraints. To counter dilution, BadTime leverages inter-variable correlations, temporal lags, and data-driven initialization to design a distributed, lag-aware trigger whose influence persists over long-range forecasts. To overcome sparsity, it mixes label-similar and label-dissimilar poisoned samples and adopts a decoupled backdoor training objective that explicitly up-weights the sparse backdoor signal, enabling reliable learning at a poisoning rate as low as 1%. Extensive experiments show that BadTime significantly outperforms the state-of-the-art backdoor attack on time series forecasting by extending the attackable horizon from at most 12 timesteps to 720 timesteps (a 60x improvement) and reducing the MAE on target variables by 57% on average, while changing the average clean forecasting error by less than 1%.</p></details> |  |
| **[STeP-TS: Shared Temporal Filters with Pruning for Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35128v2)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multivariate time series forecasting requires capturing temporal dependencies across multiple scales while accounting for both shared and variable-specific dynamics. Compact forecasting models have emerged as an attractive alternative to increasingly large architectures, but preserving strong predictive performance within tight computational and memory budgets remains challenging. We propose a compact one-layer convolutional framework that learns a shared bank of multi-scale temporal filters of each variable. Lightweight variable-specific scalar weights determine the contribution of each temporal component, enabling the model to capture shared temporal structure while adapting to variable-specific dynamics. The learned weights and filter-output energy further provide a natural signal for structured pruning of convolutional filters, reducing computational complexity with minimal degradation in forecasting performance. We additionally introduce an amplitude-adaptive normalization mechanism that preserves variable-dependent temporal amplitudes. Experiments on multiple datasets demonstrate forecasting performance competitive with state-of-the-art methods while requiring substantially fewer parameters and inference FLOPs and achieving low per-sample latency.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the Workshop TS-LIMITS at NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Foreclassing: From Forecasts to Decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04956v3)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Time series forecasts are widely used to inform decisions under uncertainty. In many applications, these decisions involve classifying a time series based on outcomes that have yet to be observed. In this paper, we introduce Foreclassing, a new machine learning problem in which the class label of a time series is defined as a function of its future observations. We provide a mathematical formulation of Foreclassing and establish several theoretical results characterizing the problem. These results describe how additional future information affects Bayes classification risk, provide bounds on the classification error of learned Foreclassing models, and demonstrate how future observations used during training can yield finite-sample improvements over conventional classifiers. To provide a benchmark for future research, we propose ForeClassNet, a family of neural network models that jointly perform forecasting and classification while accounting for uncertainty in future outcomes. We investigate a variety of model architectures and evaluate their performance against conventional time series classifiers and forecasting-then-classify approaches. Experiments on datasets spanning weather, energy, and finance demonstrate the potential of Foreclassing to improve classification performance, probabilistic prediction, and downstream decision-making. Together, these theoretical and empirical contributions establish a foundation for future research on Foreclassing.</p></details> | <details><summary>45 pa...</summary><p>45 pages, 1 figure, 24 tables</p></details> |
| **[Analysis of Uncertain GARCH Model with the application of crude oil future prices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37071v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Time series data often exhibit variation, clustering, and persistence in uncertain environments. Based on an uncertain measure, existing studies have primarily focused on conditional mean structures while ignoring variance and persistent dynamics. To address this gap, this paper develops an uncertain generalized autoregressive conditional heteroskedasticity (UGARCH) model that incorporates lagged conditional variance terms. We first prove sufficient conditions to ensure mean stationarity of the variance process. Then, we propose recursive constrained least squares to estimate the model parameters and use cross validation to determine the orders of the mean and variance equations. In addition, we also employ uncertain hypothesis tests to determine whether a time series exhibits a UGARCH effect and to assess the goodness of fit. Point and interval forecasts are subsequently constructed from the recursively obtained next-period conditional variance and the expected value of the mean equation. Numerical simulations demonstrate the parameter estimation performance of the proposed method and show that UGARCH achieves smaller conditional-variance fitting errors than UARCH under the considered settings. In the empirical analysis, we apply the proposed method to West Texas Intermediate crude oil futures closing prices, which exhibit time-varying volatility and support short-term point and interval forecasts. Keywords: Uncertain measure, Uncertain time-series analysis, UGARCH model, hypothesis test</p></details> |  |
| **[Identifying and disentangling direct and spillover effects using multiple pre-treatment periods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37049v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Under interference, the treatment of some units affects the outcomes of others, so that the effect on a treated unit consists of a direct and a spillover component. A non-treated comparison group then generally identifies neither component, but the difference between the total effect on the treated and the spillover effect on the comparison units. We show that both components are identified separately when multiple pre-treatment periods are available, without an uncontaminated comparison group. Under no anticipation, pre-treatment outcomes are free of both components for every unit. Extrapolating the pre-treatment trend within a group, as in interrupted time series approaches, therefore identifies the counterfactual outcome in the absence of any treatment. Applied to the treated it gives the total effect on them, applied to the non-treated at a given level of exposure the spillover effect on them. Disentangling the two components in addition requires effect homogeneity across treated and non-treated units with the same exposure and covariates, as well as common support in exposure. We derive doubly robust and Neyman-orthogonal expressions for the effects of interest, which permits controlling for covariates by machine learning, and establish asymptotic normality. A simulation study confirms the root-$n$ behaviour of the estimators and the reliability of the standard errors. We apply the framework to the compulsory licensing of enemy-owned patents in the United States in 1919. Licensing raised patenting in the licensed subclasses and in technologically close ones alike, so that a comparison-group design understates the total effect.</p></details> |  |
| **[Ground-to-Satellite Knowledge Distillation for Crop Classification under Temporal and Geographic Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37021v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Continuous and up-to-date crop type mapping is essential for agricultural monitoring but is often hampered by limited reference data. The growing availability of ground-level field photographs offers a promising way to address this challenge. When combined with satellite time series, these images provide complementary perspectives on the same parcel, capturing crop characteristics from distinctly different perspectives. However, field photographs are not always available for each satellite pixel at inference time, limiting their potential in scalable crop type mapping. We investigate whether ground photographs can provide privileged supervision for crop classification built on pretrained satellite embeddings. A photo teacher based on frozen CLIP features supervises classifiers trained on AlphaEarth and TESSERA representations; photographs are used only during training. On a paired field photo and satellite imagery dataset with 17 crop classes, knowledge distillation consistently improves over satellite-only classification; for frozen embeddings the margin grows under temporal shift, reaching up to +5.5 macro-F1 points, and widens in geographic holdouts (+3.7 vs +3.0 in-distribution). These results show that ground-view supervision can complement satellite embeddings and improve their robustness without changing inference requirements.</p></details> |  |
| **[On the Tradeoffs of On-Device Generative Models in Federated Predictive Maintenance Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07860v2)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Federated Learning (FL) has emerged as a promising paradigm for preserving client data ownership and control over distributed Internet of Things (IoT) environments. While discriminative models dominate most FL use cases, recent advances in generative models - such as Variational Autoencoders (VAE), Generative Adversarial Networks (GAN), and Diffusion Models (DM) - offer new opportunities for unsupervised anomaly detection in time series analysis, with relevant applications in predictive maintenance (PdM) in critical industrial infrastructures. In this work, we present a comprehensive analysis of VAEs, GANs, and DMs in the context of federated PdM. We analyze their performance and communication overhead under both full and partial federation setups, where only subsets of model components are shared. Building on split-parameter personalized FL, this paper provides an empirical instantiation of shared/private parameter partitioning for unsupervised generative Time-Series Anomaly Detection (TSAD), mapping the split to analysis and synthesis modules across the generative architectures. Our experiments over five clients and two industrial time-series benchmarks reveal distinct trade-offs in model utility, stability, and communication efficiency in heterogeneous cross-silo FL. For the evaluated GAN-based configurations, full federation improves final anomaly-detection utility relative to independent local training, although these models remain less robust than the VAE- and DDPM-based alternatives. For DMs, decoder sharing matches full federation while substantially reducing the communication payload.</p></details> | <details><summary>Publi...</summary><p>Published in IEEE Internet of Things Journal, 2026</p></details> |
| **[Multi-task Disentangling Fusion Mamba-CNN for MODIS Spectral-Temporal Unmixing with Endmember Variability Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36750v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Spectral-temporal unmixing of MODIS time series is essential for applications such as abundance mapping and spectral library construction, yet it remains challenging due to (i) spectral-temporal information coupling in MODIS data, (ii) intra-class endmember spectral variability and inter-class signature ambiguity, and (iii) the instability of naively fusing multiple expert representations. This paper presents the Multi- task Disentangling Fusion Mamba (MDF-Mamba) to address these limitations. To resolve spectral-temporal coupling, we design a disentangling architecture that pairs per-channel spectral experts with a dedicated bidirectional state-space (Mamba) temporal branch. Convolutional modules each process a single spectral channel, while the Mamba branch encodes the cross-channel seasonal trajectory, forming a multi-branch expert ensemble with soft inter-expert reweighting based on squeeze-and-excitation. For the integration of these spectral and temporal expert outputs, we introduce mHCFusion, adapting the manifold-constrained hyper-connection framework to merge the outputs in a stable and interpretable manner. Endmember variability is addressed by extending the linear mixing model with pixel-wise learnable spectral variability weights that capture intra-class endmember variation. Finally, we design a multi-task training objective that combines supervised abundance estimation with self-supervised spectral reconstruction and endmember regularization terms on the same training data, yielding a highly efficient framework for spectral-temporal unmixing. Experiments on MODIS time series covering the Alberta and Saskatchewan provinces show that MDF-Mamba achieves the best performance on 12 of 14 evaluation metrics across both datasets (7 of 7 on Alberta; 5 of 7 on Saskatchewan), ranking second on the remaining two.</p></details> |  |
| **[RankFormer: Rethinking Time Series Transformers through Representation Capacity Analysis and Progressive Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36703v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multivariate time series forecasting plays a critical role in Web and cyber-physical systems, where large-scale services and IoT infrastructures generate complex temporal data. Transformer-based models have achieved strong empirical performance in this domain, yet the principles governing the expressive capacity of their learned representations remain insufficiently understood. Moreover, many existing models rely on simplified decoding strategies, including channel-independent modeling and direct projection heads, which limit their ability to capture complex multivariate dynamics. To address these challenges, we propose RankFormer, a novel encoder-decoder architecture for multivariate time series forecasting. We establish a rank-based analysis framework to investigate how tokenization, embedding, and attention designs affect the representation capacity of time series Transformers. Our analysis reveals that patchification increases the potential representation capacity by aggregating multiple temporal observations into each token, while subsequent encoding operations determine how much of this capacity is realized. Guided by these insights, we develop a rank-enhancing encoder with Hourglass Attention to improve rank stability during information interaction. Furthermore, we introduce the Interactive Progressive Decoder (IPD), which improves future trajectory modeling through global-guided cross-channel interaction and progressive refinement. Extensive experiments on real-world long-term forecasting benchmarks demonstrate that RankFormer achieves competitive performance against state-of-the-art methods. Code repository: https://github.com/TangPeiwang/Rankformer.</p></details> | 12 pages |
| **[Modeling Dependence Structures in Astronomical Multi-Band Time Series Data via Multi-Output Gaussian Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21431v2)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern astronomical time-domain surveys routinely collect multi-band light curves that provide complementary information about the physical processes governing source variability. Although various multi-output Gaussian process (GP) models have been applied in different astronomical contexts, the statistical and astrophysical implications of their dependence structures have received comparatively limited attention. In this work, we examine three existing approaches to modeling cross-band dependence within a common multi-output GP framework and investigate how their underlying assumptions affect statistical inference and astrophysical interpretation. These include covariance-based, stochastic differential equation (SDE)-based, and latent-process approaches, which differ mainly in how cross-band dependence is introduced. The covariance-based approach directly specifies cross-band covariance functions to characterize dependence, whereas the SDE-based approach specifies joint temporal dynamics, with cross-band dependence arising from coupled dynamics or correlated driving processes. The latent-process approach represents dependence through shared stochastic processes and their transformations, which can reflect physical relationships among multiple light curves. We demonstrate the practical implications of these modeling choices through applications to multi-band active galactic nucleus light curves and emission-line reverberation mapping. Rather than advocating a universally preferred approach, this work provides guidance for selecting dependence structures according to scientific objectives and clarifies how these choices influence the statistical characterization and scientific interpretation of multi-band stochastic variability.</p></details> | 20 pages, 5 figures |
| **[Learning from Points and Patches: A Pre-trained Hybrid Attention Transformer for Time Series Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36572v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Time-series data generated by Web services and connected systems support applications such as user activity analysis, service monitoring, and resource management. However, learning transferable representations from heterogeneous time series remains challenging due to diverse temporal patterns, variable sequence lengths, and limited high-quality annotations. Despite the success of pre-training in natural language processing and computer vision, effective time-series pre-training requires strategies tailored to temporal data. We propose TimeHAT (Hybrid Attention Transformer), a general-purpose pre-training framework for time-series analysis that addresses two complementary questions: what to learn and how to learn. For what to learn, we introduce a Unified Point-Patch Masking Strategy (UPPMS) that integrates point-level and patch-level supervision within a unified framework for contextual reconstruction and causal extrapolation, enabling the model to capture both fine-grained temporal variations and broader temporal patterns. For how to learn, we develop a Hybrid Attention Encoder (HAE) that combines prefix-constrained bidirectional attention in initial layers with directional hybrid attention in deeper layers, aiming to preserve effective contextual interactions while mitigating rank degeneration. We evaluate TimeHAT across real-world datasets and four downstream tasks: classification, long-term forecasting, imputation, and anomaly detection. Experiments cover fine-tuning, few-shot, and zero-shot transfer settings, demonstrating competitive performance across diverse time-series analysis tasks. Code is available at https://github.com/TangPeiwang/TimeHAT.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages. Code: https://github.com/TangPeiwang/TimeHAT</p></details> |
| **[ATEMPO: Amortized Transfer Entropy Model Per-lag Over-Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36536v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>When one time series informs another, the delay at which that information arrives determines if models can utilize this relationship effectively. A source may be strongly predictive at one delay but uninformative at neighboring lags. Therefore, fixed input windows may obscure this structure by bundling informative and irrelevant history together. Transfer entropy (TE) can recover these delays by measuring how much a source's past tells us about a target beyond the target's own history. However, existing TE estimators require separate fits across source and conditioning depths, with further estimation on local windows to track delays over time. We introduce ATEMPO, a diffusion-based transfer entropy estimator amortized over lags and time. ATEMPO samples source and conditioning depths during training and conditions explicitly on temporal position, allowing a single score network to estimate the full TE surface without refitting. Differencing this surface recovers when information arrives. Across synthetic datasets with ground-truth TE, ATEMPO achieves the lowest mean relative error among baselines, and on datasets with known lags, ATEMPO achieves $0.98\pm0.04$ lag-detection accuracy. ATEMPO requires a single fit across source depths $K=6$ and target depths $L=3$, whereas existing estimators require 18 separate fits to evaluate the same $K \times L$ grid. On three systems with time-varying lag, ATEMPO is the only estimator to maintain perfect lag-detection accuracy across all evaluated window lengths. These recovered delays improve downstream forecasting: ATEMPO-selected inputs reduce MSE on WEATHER-5K by 7.25%, while 120 selected inputs outperform the benchmark's 240-feature default by 4.6%. Across three brain-computer interface tasks, using ATEMPO-selected lags achieves the highest or equal-best $R^2$, improving mean $R^2$ by 4.1% relative to the strongest competing baseline.</p></details> |  |
| **[Inferring Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.03186v4)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Patterns of microcircuitry in the cerebral cortex suggest that the brain has an array of repeated canonical computational units. However, neural representations are distributed, so the relevant computations may only be related indirectly to single-neuron transformations. It thus remains an open challenge how to define canonical distributed computations. Here we integrate normative and algorithmic theories of neural computation to present a mathematical framework for inferring canonical distributed computations from large-scale neural activity patterns. At the normative level, we hypothesize that the brain learns a structured internal model of its environment and its latent causes. At the algorithmic level, we propose that this inference process is a nonlinear message-passing algorithm on a graph-structured model of the world. Given a time series of neural activity during a perceptual inference task, our analysis framework finds (i) the neural representation of the relevant latent variables, (ii) interactions between these latent variables that define the brain's internal model of the world, and (iii) message functions that specify the inference algorithm. With enough data, key computational properties are identifiable due to constraints from the symmetries inherent in any canonical computation, up to a joint global transformation of all interactions and the message-passing functions. As a demonstration of this framework, we analyze artificial neural recordings generated by a simulated brain that implicitly implements an approximate inference algorithm. Given simulated inputs and neural activity we successfully recover the latent variables, their neural representation and dynamics, and canonical message functions that govern the dynamics. Analysis of these models reveals features of experimental design required to extract canonical computations from neural data.</p></details> | <details><summary>26 pa...</summary><p>26 pages, 4 figures and 1 supplementary figure</p></details> |
| **[Mirror Likelihood for Modeling Manifold Data with Application on Correlation Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36448v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Manifold-valued data arise in a wide range of scientific applications. Existing statistical methods typically follow either an intrinsic approach, which characterizes the manifold itself via quantities such as geodesic distance or the Fréchet mean, or an extrinsic approach, which embeds the manifold into an ambient Euclidean space, performs unconstrained statistical estimation, and projects the result back to the manifold. While the latter is conceptually and computationally simple, likelihood-based extrinsic solutions remain scarce, due to the lack of a general recipe for choosing an embedding map and for characterizing its impact on the transformed distribution. In this article, we tackle the problem using an augmented likelihood approach: we view the manifold-valued data as the Fenchel mirror map of a latent variable, which is nearly unconstrained, and model the latent variable with a canonical distribution, such as Gaussian. The key transform, the Fenchel mirror map, is a generalization of the Euclidean projection and can effectively map into the interior of the manifold. Taking a Bayesian approach, the proposed framework enjoys a generative model interpretation and an augmented likelihood free from intractable normalization constants, and it supports principled uncertainty quantification and flexible model-based extensions. We study the theoretical and computational properties of the framework and demonstrate its utility in analyzing neuroimaging data of high-dimensional correlation time series, where each correlation matrix is rank deficient.</p></details> |  |
| **[Improving causal inference in interrupted time series analysis: the triple difference design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.17281v3)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Background: Interrupted time series analysis (ITSA) is widely used to evaluate health policy and intervention effects. While multiple-group ITSA (MG-ITSA) improves causal inference by incorporating a control group, residual confounding from unmeasured time-varying factors may remain. The triple-difference interrupted time series (DDD-ITSA) design extends this approach by adding a second control group to further isolate treatment effects, but it remains underutilized and lacks formal guidance. Methods: We formalize the DDD-ITSA framework, specify the regression model, define key parameters for estimating level and trend effects, and clarify interpretation of the triple-difference estimand. We illustrate the approach using a worked example evaluating California's Proposition 99 cigarette tax and its impact on per-capita cigarette sales. Results: In the example, all groups were balanced on pre-intervention level and trend. The triple-difference estimand indicated a statistically significant annual reduction of -1.76 per-capita cigarette packs in California relative to the secondary control (P = 0.020; 95 percent CI: -3.24, -0.28), consistent with results from the primary comparison. Differences between control groups were not significant. Conclusions: DDD-ITSA strengthens causal inference when two-group comparisons may be confounded by leveraging an additional control group to remove remaining biases and assess heterogeneity. Implementation is facilitated by updates to the itsa Stata package. Careful attention to control selection, baseline balance, and autocorrelation remains essential.</p></details> |  |
| **[Beta Regression with Autoregressive Errors for Interrupted Time Series Analysis of Proportion and Rate Outcomes: A Simulation Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07914v2)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Interrupted time series analyses (ITSA) of proportion and rate outcomes are frequently estimated using ordinary least squares regression despite the bounded nature of these outcomes. When methods appropriate for bounded outcomes are used, the standard approach is a quasi-likelihood generalized linear model (GLM) with heteroskedasticity- and autocorrelation-consistent (HAC) standard errors. However, no existing estimator jointly models the beta-distributed conditional density and autoregressive (AR) error structure. We introduce betark, a Stata implementation of a joint conditional maximum likelihood estimator for beta regression with AR(k) errors based on a recursive substitution that yields a closed-form conditional beta likelihood with autoregressive dependence of arbitrary order. Unlike two-stage approaches, betark jointly estimates the mean, precision, and AR(k) coefficients in a single likelihood, so reported standard errors directly account for autocorrelation without separate correction. A Monte Carlo study compared betark with a quasi-binomial GLM using Newey-West HAC standard errors across AR(1)-AR(3) processes, three series lengths, and four effect sizes in a single-group ITSA design. Both methods were essentially unbiased, but betark produced better-calibrated inference than GLM+HAC in most scenarios, with the largest gains under highly persistent autocorrelation, where GLM+HAC Type I error exceeded 60% for short series. Misspecifying the AR order by one lag and varying the starting mean and pre-intervention trend had only modest effects on performance. However, betark's own Type I error remained elevated under highly persistent AR(3) processes even for the longest series examined.</p></details> |  |
| **[A Mechanistic Interpretation of Prescribed-Parameter Convolutional Kernels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.17061v2)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Prescribed-parameter convolutional transforms achieve strong time series classification performance using fixed kernels, simple pooling operations, and a linear classifier. Their success raises a mechanistic question: which properties of the representation make this construction effective? We study the proportion of positive values (PPV), a pooling operator shared by ROCKET, MiniROCKET, and MultiROCKET. We establish exact invariance to circular translations under periodic boundaries and quantify departures from invariance under finite boundaries. A complementary analysis bounds the sensitivity of a smooth PPV surrogate. We then define the population pairwise geometry induced by a distribution of convolutional parameters and show how a finite random kernel bank concentrates around that geometry. This separates the role of the parameter distribution from the number of sampled kernels. Experiments on the UCR classification collection examine effective dimensionality, distance correlation with the input, and classification accuracy using PPV-only, unit-energy ROCKET kernels. Increasing kernel count produces diminishing representational gains. Increasing kernel length generally raises effective dimensionality and distance correlation, while the best test performance occurs at intermediate lengths and training accuracy remains close to perfect. Together, these results support an interpretation in which nonlinear pooling creates a richer but structured representation that can favour linear separation, while further representational richness need not improve generalisation.</p></details> | <details><summary>17 pa...</summary><p>17 pages, 3 figures. Substantially revised version with a new title. Replaces the sparsity-based interpretation with an analysis of PPV representations through translation invariance, concentration of random-feature geometry, and expanded experiments on kernel number and length</p></details> |
| **[CLOSURE: Cross-Channel Closure for Multivariate Time Series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35753v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multivariate time series produced by industrial installations and server clusters are under continuous monitoring, and anomaly detection is the first line of defense before a local fault escalates into a system-wide failure. In the absence of labels, the dominant paradigm trains a reconstruction model on normal data and scores the residual to reach a verdict. Recent work has steadily enriched the expressiveness of the recovery map through Transformers, diffusion models, and cross-scale associations. A residual, however, only certifies that an observation can be recovered; it does not certify that the variables still explain one another as they do under normal operation. A coordinated yet unfamiliar operating transition inflates the residual without breaking any relationship, whereas a sensor drifting away from its peers may leave the residual silent. Methods that fold inter-variable dependencies into the reconstruction objective inherit this ambiguity, because their relational evidence is ultimately supervised by the same recovery loss. We therefore propose CLOSURE, which evaluates recoverability and cross-channel consistency as two normal-data prediction tasks learned in separation. The recoverability task is solved by a diffusion reconstructor conditioned on a discrete memory of normal temporal bases. The consistency task is solved by a relation-closure operator that predicts every channel exclusively from the others, where the target's own trajectory is withheld and sparse signed gates decide which peers are actually used. The two scores are reconciled only through calibration on normal data. Extensive experiments show that CLOSURE consistently outperforms recent reconstruction-, frequency-, and topology-based detectors on public benchmark streams, confirming the value of examining consistency alongside recoverability.</p></details> |  |
| **[TRACE: Memory-Efficient Time Series Class-Incremental Learning via Temporal-Spectral Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35727v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Web-connected sensing applications continuously generate multivariate time series with evolving behavioral categories, making Time Series Class-Incremental Learning (TSCIL) essential for adapting to new classes without forgetting previously acquired knowledge. However, preserving diverse and evolving class distributions under strict privacy and memory constraints remains a fundamental challenge. Existing approaches often face a trade-off between retaining sufficient historical knowledge for accurate recognition and minimizing the memory required for continual adaptation. To address these limitations, we propose TRACE, a memory-efficient, replay-free framework that preserves class-specific knowledge through generative modeling rather than sample rehearsal. TRACE employs a compact, frozen temporal-spectral representation and lightweight class-specific autoencoders to capture complex intra-class variations. By independently preserving each class model, TRACE avoids recursive generation and catastrophic forgetting, while enabling direct classification through reconstruction-based scoring without retaining historical samples or large statistical matrices. Extensive experiments across five TSCIL benchmarks demonstrate that TRACE achieves competitive classification accuracy while reducing mean retained memory by approximately 98.26% compared with TS-ACL, the strongest evaluated analytical baseline, and achieving an approximately 33.43% relative improvement in accuracy on GrabMyo.</p></details> | <details><summary>9 pag...</summary><p>9 pages, 5 figures, 4 tables</p></details> |

Fast beats ALP on ratio and both speeds at once: 7.01$\times$ against 4.19$\times$, 476 against 120 MB/s, and 5.0 against 4.5 GB/s on one core. Upper is the smallest output on all 48 larger streams. EVOLVE models uncertainty by sampling multiple latent historical states and decoding through complementary smooth and deviation-amplifying evolution hypotheses, and the disagreement among decoded future trajectories is used to estimate future anomaly risk. | **[FinVerse: Toward More Realistic Evaluation for Financial Time-Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03259v3)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Time-series foundation models (TSFMs) are increasingly evaluated on broad benchmarks that apply uniform conventional forecast evaluation metrics across heterogeneous time-series. | **[GAS-PFN: Graph-Aware Spatiotemporal Prior-Data Fitted Networks for Zero-Shot Traffic Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37598v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Traffic forecasting requires modeling complex temporal dynamics and the spatial dependencies imposed by road topology. Supervised graph-based models achieve strong performance, but are often trained for a particular dataset and sensor graph, making deployment to new or evolving networks costly. Prior-Data Fitted Networks (PFNs) offer a zero-shot alternative by learning in-context inference from synthetic task priors, which provide an explicit mechanism for encoding domain-specific assumptions. Existing forecasting PFNs, however, do not explicitly represent the graph-structured dependencies governing traffic dynamics. To combine graph-aware traffic modeling with zero-shot adaptation, we introduce the Graph-Aware Spatiotemporal Prior-Data Fitted Network (GAS-PFN). GAS-PFN incorporates sampled road topology into its synthetic task prior through random-walk diffusion, teaching the model to learn graph-coupled dynamics during pretraining. Its co-designed inference architecture uses two mask-conditioned passes through a shared Transformer backbone to separately model sensor-specific temporal dynamics and spatiotemporal interactions among neighboring sensors before integrating their representations for prediction. This design enables GAS-PFN to use target-network structure and context without parameter updates. Across six benchmarks at the 90-minute horizon, GAS-PFN reduces MSE by 34.0% on average relative to TimePFN, its closest PFN-based predecessor. It also reduces MSE by 27.7% on average relative to four recent industry-developed general-purpose zero-shot forecasters. In this comparison, all models receive 90 minutes of contiguous recent history, and any additional context is drawn from each dataset's chronological training split. These results indicate that aligning PFN priors and inference architectures with graph structure improves in-context learning for zero-shot traffic forecasting.</p></details> |  |

Peregrine updates ML features over all traffic at line rate in a programmable switch, then downsamples the feature records sent to software inference. Across 84 attack traces from three datasets, we show that approximate features computed over all packets can outperform exact features computed from sampled packets. In our evaluation, Peregrine preserves detection effectiveness while reducing feature-record rates by three orders of magnitude - even where pre-feature sampling causes detection to collapse.</p></details> |  |

A dedicated graph neural network (GNN) first scores road segments for each OD flow using the road network and vehicle-flow information. The GNN thus guides the reduction of the road network, while the solver enforces feasibility and determines the routing. | **[Governing Enterprise AI Traffic at Scale: Design and Operation of a Policy-Driven, Multi-Vendor LLM Gateway on Envoy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35509v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large organizations consume large language models (LLMs) from several vendors at once, each with its own API dialect, credential model, streaming protocol and pricing. Integrated directly, this heterogeneity yields credential sprawl, format lock-in, unbounded spend and inconsistent governance. We present the design and production operation of the Cosmos LLM Gateway, the governed entry point for enterprise LLM traffic at PayPal. Built on Envoy, it splits work by latency profile: I/O-bound control decisions (authentication, credential brokering, policy and budget evaluation) run in an out-of-process external-processing service, while wire-format translation, token metering and prompt-cache shaping run in-proxy as a Rust WebAssembly filter, so that, by default, streamed response body chunks never wait on an out-of-process call. Models, providers and prices are largely validated data rather than code. Governance is declarative policy evaluated by Open Policy Agent and managed through a control plane with role-scoped authoring, approvals, pilots, shadow-mode rollout, usage notifications and versioned policy bundles. For use cases that require it, a data-loss-prevention layer can block, redact or pseudonymize detected sensitive content before it reaches a provider. Spend scopes are views over per-model usage counters, so a new budget applies to spend already recorded in its window without migration. As of September 2026, the gateway serves more than 12,000 developers and hundreds of business use cases, handling roughly 200 billion tokens per day across more than 100 models at 99.999% availability to business. In a benchmark of the core request path against a synthetic upstream, it adds a median of 4 ms and a p99 of 11 ms of overhead at more than 2,200 requests per second on two 4-vCPU instances. We report the architecture, policy model, evaluation and lessons learned.</p></details> | <details><summary>17 pa...</summary><p>17 pages, 6 figures, 6 tables. Industry case study</p></details> |
| **[Routing Around the Queue: An Evaluation of Eco-Routing Protocol on different Traffic Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35479v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We evaluate ErouVe, a vehicular eco-routing protocol in which roadside units (RSUs) collect per-segment travel-time and CO2 reports from passing vehicles and answer route requests with the lowest-cost alternative, across a range of road architectures and traffic scenarios. This evaluation reveals clear strengths and weaknesses of the protocol. Under light, uncongested traffic, ErouVe's fleet-wide effect is light. Under traffic dense enough to stress the signalized route's throughput and produce a real, growing queue, the same protocol succeeds a medium fleet-wide CO2 reduction, with diverted vehicles individually saving even more fuels. We use these findings to propose three targeted corrective methods; a knowledge-gated cost-acceptance rule, a diversion-locking rule, and a distance-normalized CO2 cost function, that respectively address a cold-start knowledge asymmetry, decision flapping under noisy crowd-sourced data, and a per-second CO2 metric that rewards idling. Experiments on nuPlan and Waymo demonstrate that AutoWorld improves generation realism, with larger gains in partially observed scenarios where trajectory abstractions are most limited, reducing collision rates by 36%. | **[SAGE: A hybrid vision-language and geometry framework for work-zone pathway prediction under temporary traffic control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31835v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Temporary traffic control redirects vehicles through work zones, requiring automated vehicles to connect an interpretation of the visible devices to a continuous passing path. | **[Do Flow-Generation Tools Matter? | **[Metropolis-Scale Resilient and Trustworthy Traffic Flow Inference Using Multi-Source Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25004v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Inferring network-wide traffic states from sparse observations with high accuracy and trustworthy uncertainty quantification is essential for intelligent transportation systems, yet it remains challenging due to the underdetermined nature of the problem, multifaceted disturbances in sensing networks, and the inherent conflicts among multiple inference subtasks when modeled jointly. For uncertainty quantification, we combine neural processes with Monte Carlo Dropout to capture both aleatoric and epistemic uncertainty. This work distills and protects traffic semantics during video editing at three levels. First, we construct a hybrid dataset by combining temporally aligned real-world videos with synthetic edits, balancing foundational scene structure with unambiguous appearance signals. Second, during training, we adapt advantage conditioning from offline reinforcement learning to align the model using dense rewards. We extract dense, pixel-level rewards from the hybrid data to serve as a localized signal and actively steer generation toward structurally reliable pixels. Finally, our model mitigates temporal shock by introducing thinking frames, a transition mechanism that seamlessly reconciles static image conditioning with dynamic video context. | **[BR-MoE: Budgeted Semantic Release for Privacy-Preserving Traffic Analytics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27026v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Traffic cameras support useful analysis, but their outputs can reveal private information. Replacing images with text reduces direct visual exposure. It does not place a formal bound on disclosure. The published reinforcement learning and mixture-of-experts framework refines scene descriptions through relevance, coverage, and brevity rewards. It does not account for repeated releases under a fixed privacy budget. This paper proposes budgeted semantic release as an extension of that framework. Four scene experts first process isolated video chunks. Their outputs are mapped to a fixed, bounded semantic schema. A reinforcement learning controller selects which expert summaries to release and how much privacy budget to spend. The controller uses public task requests and previous private releases. A separate privacy filter enforces the total budget. Calibrated noise is added before the summaries are converted to text. Under explicit chunk isolation and bounded-event assumptions, the complete release transcript satisfies an event-level differential privacy bound. The design also supports reuse of previous summaries without new disclosure. An evaluation protocol covers traffic utility, privacy attacks, release freshness, and computational cost. Illustrative tables show the intended reporting format. They do not establish empirical gains. The proposed contribution is a task-aware release interface with auditable privacy accounting for repeated traffic descriptions.</p></details> |  |
| **[Edge Deployable Lightweight Framework for Real-Time Driver Safety System with Integrated Lane Departure and Traffic Sign Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25340v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Lane departure and traffic-sign negligence are leading contributors to road accidents, particularly in regions with non-standardized signage and limited annotated data. | **[Spectral Adaptive Graph Transformer for Efficient and Scalable Traffic Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25140v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Traffic forecasting models based on spectral graph convolution offer an interpretable account of how information is mixed between sensors, but they rely on an eigendecomposition of the graph Laplacian. | **[MAppPrint: Robust and Real-time Mobile App Fingerprinting via Traffic Behavior Correlation Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22148v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Mobile apps have become ubiquitous in daily life. Furthermore, MAppPrint employs an unsupervised decomposition strategy to disentangle mixed traffic generated by multi-app concurrency, enabling unified identification of both single-app and multi-app traffic. | **[SceneFactory-3D: Lifting 2D Traffic Scenes into 3D Physical Counterfactuals for Scalable Physically Grounded Safety Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02874v2)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Scalable driving simulators typically execute vehicle commands using prescribed behavioral or kinematic rules, overlooking the physics of tire-road interfaces, thereby limiting their ability to capture how adverse road and environmental conditions alter vehicle execution and propagate through traffic. Code: https://github.com/SmallWorldLab/SceneFactory_3D</p></details> |  |
| **[NetCoFi: A Budget-Aware Coarse-Fine Dual-Branch Model for Encrypted Traffic Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17978v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Encrypted traffic classification is critical for network management and security, but practical deployment must balance classification accuracy and inference cost. Existing models typically process fixed flow windows with limited control over computation after training, which can limit the accuracy-efficiency trade-off under constrained resources. To address this problem, we present NetCoFi, a budget-aware classifier that allocates computation at coarse and fine granularities. Using byte representations and observable packet metadata, NetCoFi selects token neighborhoods for fine-grained encoding while compressing the full window into complementary coarse context. Multi-budget training enables the same model to operate at different routing budgets without retraining. Evaluation on six public datasets shows that NetCoFi achieves state-of-the-art classification performance when trained from scratch. Under low-budget settings, NetCoFi improves average Macro-F1 by 7.86 percentage points across three datasets, compared with a same-depth static baseline. | **[Shortcut in Generalization: Rethinking Evaluation Protocol for Encrypted Traffic Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17961v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Encrypted traffic classification is commonly evaluated under a range of experimental protocols, with classification performance reported as the primary measure. However, the reported performance may be influenced by shortcuts left uncontrolled in the evaluation protocol, such as shortcut features and cross-partition sharing, rather than reflect generalizable traffic patterns. And test-only feature transformations may further introduce train-test mismatch. In this paper, we study how current evaluation protocols are affected by shortcut-related factors, including field-level shortcuts, cross-partition sharing, and train-test processing. We compare feature importance, retrained feature removal, and field-only split sensitivity. We also vary Source-IP overlap and packets per flow, compare test-only IP transformations with matched train-test transformations. Experiments on three public datasets show that the performance of existing methods are affected by both cross-partition sharing and the consistency of train-test transformations. Specifically, increasing Source-IP overlap from 0% to 100% raises accuracy by about 32-33 points before identifier masking, compared with only about 8 points after masking. For two state-of-the-art pretrained models (i.e., YaTC and NetMamba), the 11-13 points loss caused by test-only IP zeroing is almost fully recovered when the same transformation is applied during both training and testing.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted for publication at IEEE CSCloud 2026</p></details> |
| **[SIGMA: Symmetry-aware, Intelligent, Geometric, Multi-faced Adaptive Traffic Control for Reliable Intelligent Transportation Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18263v2)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Traffic signal control is a challenging sequential decision-making problem requiring a balance between throughput, vehicle delay fairness, signal stability, predictable switching, and emergency vehicle prioritisation. Existing reinforcement learning (RL) approaches often rely on predefined objectives and offer limited adaptability to changing operational priorities. To address these limitations, SIGMA (Symmetry-aware, Intelligent, Geometric, Multi-faced Adaptive traffic control) is proposed as a multi-faced RL framework augmented with a large language model (LLM) for dynamic objective adaptation and orientation-invariant policy learning. Natural language emergency instructions are translated into priority vectors that guide a multi-faced actor-critic controller. Symmetry-aware rotational augmentation supports transfer across homogeneous four-way intersections, while an offline-to-online learning strategy enables stable initialisation and adaptation to evolving traffic conditions. Structural properties concerning emergency service levels, graceful degradation under LLM failure, and sensitivity to traffic demand perturbations are established, alongside bootstrap-based statistical validation. Experiments in SUMO across four urban intersections modelled on Kolkata traffic scenarios compare SIGMA with fixed-time, actuated, and Deep Q-Network controllers. Reductions in average waiting times, emergency waiting times, and queue lengths are observed, alongside improved traffic throughput. Empirical reliability and ablation analyses further assess robustness to component failures and geometric orientation. SIGMA integrates language-guided objective adaptation, multi-faced policy learning, and statistical reliability assessment into an adaptive framework for intelligent traffic control.</p></details> |  |
| **[She Spoofed Sea Ships by the Sea Shore: Measuring Large-Scale GPS Spoofing in Global Maritime Traffic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37676v2)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>GPS spoofing has emerged as a serious threat to maritime security, yet its global prevalence, persistence, and structure remain largely unmeasured. In this paper, we present the first large-scale measurement study of maritime GPS spoofing, using global Automatic Identification System (AIS) data, which contain the GPS coordinates broadcasted over time by ships across the world. We focus on large-scale regional spoofing, where external interference displaces many vessels across an area at once, leaving a recognizable signature of physically implausible motion correlated across ships; our motion-aware, marine-specific framework identifies this signature and grades the evidence for GPS spoofing in each region it finds. Applying our approach to AIS data from over 367,000 vessels collected between late November 2024 and early February 2025, we identify 31 persistent anomalous hotspots across high-traffic maritime regions, at least 22 of which show strong evidence of GPS spoofing, with spatial and temporal structure aligning with regional conflict and economic sanctions. Notably, our method found that the spoofing activity in the Red Sea responsible for the highly-publicized grounding of the 75,000-ton container ship, MSC Antonia, was ongoing months before the incident, which has not been previously documented. Similarly, we detected persistent spoofing in the Strait of Hormuz over a year before the 2026 Iran war brought commercial shipping through the Strait to near-standstill. Together, this work establishes GPS spoofing as a widespread, recurring, and measurable threat to global maritime navigation.</p></details> | <details><summary>20 pa...</summary><p>20 pages, 19 figures, To appear in IEEE Symposium on Security and Privacy 2027 (IEEE S&P)</p></details> |
| **[Risk Control of Traffic Flow Through Chance Constraints and Large Deviation Approximation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.01321v2)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We develop a rare chance-constrained optimal control framework for autonomous traffic management, with traffic dynamics governed by stochastic partial differential equations. The proposed LDT-based reformulation exhibits superior computational scalability, as its computational cost remains approximately constant regardless of the target violation probability, as opposed to the scaling challenge of traditional sampling-based methods. The effectiveness of the proposed framework is illustrated through numerical experiments on a ring road with stochastic on-ramp inflow, and evaluated with two representative risk measures, namely, maximum traffic density and speed variation. The results show that the proposed method keeps the violation probability close to the target without parameter tuning and is more than ten times faster than a risk-averse baseline.</p></details> |  |

| **[PACC: Protocol-Aware Cross-Layer Compression for Compact Network Traffic Representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.08331v2)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Network traffic classification underpins network security and management but faces growing challenges from pervasive encryption and evolving protocols. We observe substantial redundancy both across and within network layers, which existing paradigms do not explicitly address, wasting capacity and encouraging shortcut learning that degrades generalization. To address this, we propose PACC, a redundancy-aware cross-layer representation framework. PACC models protocol layers as multiview inputs and learns faithful, compact projections factorized into shared (cross-layer) and private (layer-specific) components. It incorporates an information-preserving sparse segment representation to reduce structural repetition and supervised intra-layer filtering to suppress task-irrelevant semantic redundancy. A behavior-guided shared/private decomposition aligns cross-layer consensus while retaining layer-specific information, supported by information-theoretic analysis. Across encrypted application classification, IoT device identification, benign/malicious traffic classification, and Tor traffic detection, PACC achieves the best overall performance. On encrypted traffic, it achieves an F1 score of 0.9685, substantially outperforming existing baselines while using approximately 15x fewer computes and less GPU memory than the latest pre-trained traffic model.</p></details> |  |
| **[EvoSignal: LLM-Guided Evolutionary Design of Modular Traffic Signal Control Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09563v2)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Effective traffic signal control (TSC) requires policies that respond to changing traffic demand and network conditions while meeting different control objectives. This paper formulates TSC as a modular program design problem and proposes EvoSignal, an LLM-guided evolutionary framework using traffic knowledge and performance feedback. The modular representation separates traffic feature extraction, local phase prioritization, and optional network-based priority adjustment. Starting from several established strategies, EvoSignal improves programs through feedback on congestion and signal operation, retaining strategies with different performance trade-offs. The resulting programs operate without online LLM inference. | **[FlowWise: Stateful Fast-Slow Model Serving for Streaming Traffic Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.03694v3)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Network traffic analysis in cloud and internet service provider (ISP) networks increasingly relies on machine learning (ML) models, as encryption and service consolidation make rule-based classification unreliable. This paper presents FlowWise, a serving system for streaming, stateful flow intelligence on commodity CPU/GPU servers that jointly selects (i) how many packets to collect and (ii) which model(s) to apply for each flow to balance latency, service rate, and accuracy. Across three representative tasks, inference time across candidate models differs by 1.8x-141.3x, while packet waiting time can exceed feature computation and inference by 6-8 orders of magnitude at the tail. FlowWise exploits these gaps with an uncertainty-driven fast-slow cascade: it issues early predictions from a one-packet model and escalates only uncertain flows to slower models that wait for additional packets and use richer features. A queue-centric runtime maintains per-flow state, performs asynchronous feature joining, and uses brokered, flow-ID-keyed streams to distribute workload across heterogeneous workers. FlowWise serves 76.3% of flows within 16 ms, improving median end-to-end serving latency by 40.5x while increasing service rate and maintaining similar accuracy to always running the slow model. | **[RIECNN: Real-Time Image Enhanced CNN for Traffic Sign Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15127v1)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Traffic sign recognition plays a crucial role in the development of autonomous cars to reduce the accident rate and promote road safety. | **[Answer with Evidence: Consistency-Aware Grounded Visual Question Answering for Roadside Traffic Scenes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05274v2)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Roadside traffic reasoning requires every free-form textual claim to be backed by visual evidence. | **[Semantic Edge-Cloud Communication for Real-Time Urban Traffic Digital Twins](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14187v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Urban traffic systems are becoming increasingly complex, demanding real-time traffic perception, proactive collision prediction, and intelligent management to improve safety and reduce congestion. By leveraging edge intelligence to extract and transmit only task-relevant semantic traffic information instead of full video feeds, the proposed framework significantly reduces communication overhead while preserving the essential dynamics needed for accurate real-time digital twin reconstruction. | **[Offline Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning with a Physics-Informed World Model for Cooperative Mixed Traffic Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17739v2)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This study investigates cooperative control of connected and automated vehicles (CAVs) at partially observable highway bottlenecks in mixed traffic, aiming to mitigate congestion without relying on complete global traffic states or online trial-and-error. | **[Reinforcement Learning-Based Traffic Signal Control for IoT-Enabled Intersections](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22108v2)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Urban traffic congestion remains a persistent challenge in car-dependent cities, imposing significant economic and societal costs. ## Graph Neural Networks
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Spectral Braids: A Topological Descriptor for Dynamic Directed Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37580v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Eigenvalues are a workhorse of graph machine learning: spectral clustering, spectral graph neural networks, and graph kernels all summarize a network by the eigenvalues of a matrix. These eigenvalues are almost always treated as a static list of numbers. When a graph changes, however, its eigenvalues move, and when the matrix is not symmetric, they move in the complex plane, where they can wind around each other. The pattern of this winding is a braid, a classical object from topology. We propose spectral braids, a methodology for dynamic directed networks: each snapshot is aggregated into a community-level transition matrix, its complex eigenvalues are tracked over time, and the braid they trace is read off as a word. The word is then mapped to a matrix by the Burau representation, which gives a fixed-size feature vector for any classifier. The braid has a concrete meaning: each eigenvalue describes how a group of communities behaves, and each crossing records one group overtaking another. We evaluate the method on dynamic network classification, using synthetic networks of online communities whose labels describe how the network evolved between the same starting and ending states. Our experimental results show that the proposed features reach 97.1% accuracy on four classes, against 44.5% for the best feature set that does not track eigenvalues, and that the Burau features also separate networks containing the same events in a different order, which simple counts of crossings cannot do. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first study that defines braids and braid monodromy on graph data and uses them as features for learning on dynamic networks.</p></details> | <details><summary>15 pa...</summary><p>15 pages, 5 figures, 2 tables. Accepted at the 2026 IEEE International Conference on Big Data (IEEE BigData 2026)</p></details> |
| **[GTFormer: A Syntax-Free Graph Network Alternative to Transformers for 8192$\times$ Length Extrapolation in Linear Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37524v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Self-attention mechanisms have established the Transformer as the dominant backbone across natural language processing, source code intelligence, and automated theorem proving. Inspired by recent advances in algebraic graph learning (\textbf{WPNet}), this work introduces \textbf{GTFormer}, a syntax-free hierarchical Graph Neural Network (GNN) that replaces flat self-attention for general long-context sequence modeling. GTFormer organizes raw token streams into a bounded-degree hierarchy combining a balanced binary reduction tree ($\lceil \log_2 N \rceil$ depth), horizontal boundary-bridging shift edges, random expander shortcuts, multi-channel convex highway gating, and a top-down broadcast pass. Across eight long-context code, log, and multi-hop retrieval benchmarks trained exclusively on sequences of length $N \le 512$, GTFormer extrapolates zero-shot to $N = 4{,}194{,}304$ ($8192\times$) on a single commodity GPU in linear $\mathcal{O}(N)$ memory, maintaining near-perfect accuracy where standard and RoPE Transformers suffer from out-of-distribution attention collapse and out-of-memory exhaustion.</p></details> | 20 pages |

We demonstrate its utility through expert-guided analysis scenarios involving 505 patients with breast, colorectal, and lung cancers, evaluating its ability to reveal clinically meaningful collaboration patterns. | **[Escaping the Phylogenetic Trap: Persistent Homology Enables Cross-Species Ion Channel Classification via Multimodal Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30394v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Protein classifiers are highly sensitive to evaluation protocol: random train/test splits induce phylogenetic leakage, allowing models to exploit taxon-specific correlations and substantially overestimate out-of-distribution performance. Real world transaction data for fraud detection is severely affected by class imbalance making it harder to detect these transactions. Fraudulent transactions are rarely isolated. | **[Graph Neural Networks for Astrophysics and Cosmology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27051v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Physics seldom makes breakthroughs when we consider objects only in isolation. Instead we model and measure how objects sit among their neighbours, spatial, temporal, or otherwise: a galaxy in its environment, a halo in its history, a photon hit in a detector. Graphs are ideally suited for encoding exactly these relations, and Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) learn the geometric functions that respect them. This review presents the use of GNNs for astrophysics and cosmology, and argues that GNNs often suit physics and physical data better than any alternative: they fit the native geometry of the domain and the signal, enable the incorporation of the symmetries and prior knowledge we already trust and have toiled to build, and stay flexible enough to capture what we do not yet understand. GNNs' physically intuitive workings also render them more interpretable than other deep-learning architectures. We show how we can begin from general principles of the geometry of deep learning and build the graph and the graph neural network from the ground up, and how other architectures, like the convolutional network and the transformer, can be seen as special cases of one unified geometric blueprint. We survey applications across cosmology, galaxy formation, dynamics, and instrumentation; and we chart the frontier. From this survey, we find that only one in six articles outside instrumentation applies GNNs to data rather than just simulations. We also distil a set of best practices, including when not to reach for a graph. Throughout, we will prioritise physical intuition and foundational principles over mathematical machinery whenever possible.</p></details> | <details><summary>Main ...</summary><p>Main text contains 43 pages, 17 figures, and 2 tables. Invited review chapter for the edited book Machine Learning Techniques for Astrophysics and Cosmology (Eds. Cosimo Bambi, Vinay Kashyap, Swarnim Shashank, Naoki Yoshida, Springer)</p></details> |
| **[Controllable GNN Explanations via Multi-Metric Preference Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32436v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Mechanisms for generating GNN explanations are crucial for building trust and mitigating biases in Graph Neural Networks (GNNs), especially in high-stakes scenarios. Rather than predicting the solution in a single pass, SOCPNet learns from intermediate iterates to progressively refine its estimates using a trajectory-regularized training procedure. We evaluate SOCPNet on multiple datasets spanning standard SOCP problem classes and show that it consistently reduces the final solution error relative to a SOCP-GNN baseline. NRPs extend Datalog-style rules with operations that combine, aggregate, and transform embeddings, thereby interleaving relational reasoning and learnable neural components within a single formalism. This yields a general approach to neural computation over relational data: an NRP can be read both as a query plan with trainable components and as a neural architecture with relational structure built in. Natural syntactic fragments of NRPs recover existing architectures and query formalisms. Zero-ary NRPs correspond to non-adaptive query algorithms; monadic NRPs generalize GNN-style message passing and precisely capture Deep Homomorphism Networks, a connection that we extend to frontier-guarded NRPs over databases with row-ids. We characterize the expressive power of unrestricted NRPs with ReLU-FFN transformations by FOCQ, an extension of first-order logic with counting interpreted over real-weighted structures, yielding a precise connection with uniform TC$^0$ over ordered databases. Together, these results establish NRPs as a broad declarative framework for querying and neural computation over relational data.</p></details> | <details><summary>55 pa...</summary><p>55 pages; Accepted at PODS 2026</p></details> |

To address the problem, we introduce GraphBEAT (Graph-based Bucketized Event Aggregation over Time), a temporal graph representation learning framework that groups clinical events into time buckets and transforms observed features into patient-specific, temporally directed graphs to enable early risk stratification for gastrointestinal cancers. | **[ChronoSheaf: Joint Spatio-Temporal Spectral Filtering on Cellular Sheaves](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21958v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Sheaf neural networks generalize graph neural networks by assigning each edge a learned linear restriction map that specifies how the local representations of adjacent nodes relate to one another, thereby mitigating oversmoothing. We first introduce a spectral sheaf Laplacian, defined jointly over space and time, such that a single operator regulates signal propagation across both the spatial graph and the temporal domain. Directly extending this operator's receptive field in space or time would require increasingly complex sheaf structures. Instead, we propose a spectral filter that expands the effective receptive field along both dimensions without enlarging the underlying sheaf. Building on this operator, we introduce ChronoSheaf, a spatio-temporal sheaf network with two key components. First, Transitional Restriction Maps (TRM) decompose each node's restriction map into a spatial term and two temporal frames that independently control its relation to neighboring nodes at the preceding and following timestep, enabling asymmetric temporal influence. ## Contrastive Learning
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[How Many and How Much? Contrastive Learning with Negative Samples for Knowledge Graph Embedding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37389v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Knowledge graph embedding (KGE), a fundamental technique for knowledge-centric Web and KG-augmented AI applications, is commonly trained through contrastive learning with negative samples to learn discriminative representations. Despite its empirical success, two crucial questions regarding negative samples remain underexplored: (Q1) how many negative samples should be used for each positive sample and (Q2) how much should each negative sample contribute to learning? To answer these questions, we propose RADAR, a novel contrastive learning framework for KGE models with two effective strategies, respectively: (1) Risk-aware negative budget allocation quantifies false-negative sampling risk by modeling an open-world KG as a stochastic partial observation of its closed-world counterpart and adaptively assigns a negative budget to each positive sample based on its risk; (2) Distribution-calibrated negative weighting assigns negative weights by calibrating the weighting strength for each positive based on two complementary characteristics of the negative-score distribution: dispersion and concentration. Extensive experiments on 3 real-world KGs with 4 KGE models reveal that RADAR achieves the best KGE performance on average across all model--KG combinations and reduces sampled false negatives by over 90% compared with conventional fixed-budget methods.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages, 12 figures, 5 tables</p></details> |

We introduce RISE, an inference-time semantic reranking framework that leverages label semantics to refine predictions on hard instances. RISE automatically identifies low-confidence predictions and reranks model outputs using contrastively learned label representations, without retraining or modifying the underlying model. Experiments on eight domain-specific RRL datasets with seven LMs, including encoder-based and causal architectures, show an average gain of +9.15 macro-F1 points on hard examples. For explainability, we further propose manual hardness annotations to study difficulty from both model and human perspectives, revealing a moderate agreement with Cohen's kappa = 0.40.</p></details> | <details><summary>This ...</summary><p>This submission is being withdrawn by the authors following the identification of an attribution issue. However, this extension may not fully accommodate the different modeling requirements of collaborative and multimodal information and may not be the best modeling paradigm. Our controlled experiments show that directly adopting these architectures is not the optimal modeling approach. To this end, we propose Dual-Branch Modeling with Triadic Factorization (DuTri), a framework for multimodal multi-scenario recommendation comprising a collaborative branch and a multimodal branch. Specifically, the collaborative branch learns structured collaborative preference representations through CANDECOMP/PARAFAC (CP) decomposition of user-scenario-item interactions and residual quantization, thereby capturing cross-scenario commonalities and scenario-specific differences. The multimodal branch leverages the learned user and scenario preferences to guide adaptive modality fusion; additionally, item-level contrastive learning aligns collaborative item preference representations with multimodal representations to facilitate effective collaboration between the two branches.
